# Chapter 1

## **Chapter 1**

Havana’s sugar had sweetened the tea in his study for twenty years. Now the plantation’s ghost lived in the grain of his desk, in the tremor of his hands as he signed the letter that would sell the last of it. The English roses outside his window smelled of nothing at all.




Eleanor was in the kitchen garden, her hands wrist-deep in the dark soil, teasing out the stubborn runners of bindweed from between the rows of carrots. She watched him over the low stone wall, the sun a pale coin behind the high, fast-moving clouds. She did not stand. She had learned, in the three years since her father’s slow, coughing death, that a woman alone on a farm must not appear too eager, too available. Let him come to her. The damp of the earth seeped through the knees of her old wool skirt, a familiar, grounding chill.

“Miss Chadwick?”

His voice was roughened by dust and disuse. He stood at the garden gate, holding a leather satchel as if it were a holy relic. He was younger than she’d thought, his face raw-boned and unshaven, his eyes the colour of the slate roofs in the village.

“I am she.”

“From the firm of Pargeter & Swift, London. And… further afield.” He fumbled with the clasp of the satchel, withdrawing a thick packet sealed with a daub of crimson wax. “For your hands only, miss.”

Eleanor wiped her hands on her apron, leaving smears of black loam. The wax bore the imprint of a lion passant, a crest she did not recognise. The paper was heavy, expensive, alien against her work-roughened skin. It smelled of sandalwood and salt.

“You’ve come a long way, Mr…?”

“Carter, miss. Just Carter.” He shifted his weight, his eyes darting past her to the low, stone farmhouse, its windows blank. “I am to wait for a reply, if you please.”

“Then you’d best water your horse. The pump is round the back. I’ll be in the parlour.”

The parlour was a room of quiet surrender. It held the ghost of her mother’s lavender sachets and the sharper memory of her father’s pipe tobacco, but now it was a space for damp coats and ledgers, a place where the farm’s slow, financial bleeding was charted in neat columns of red ink. Eleanor broke the seal with her thumbnail.

The letter was from a solicitor, but its subject was her uncle, Sir Alistair Chadwick. The words were a dry, legal cascade, but they boiled down to a simple, shocking fact: Alistair was dead. Not in his club in St. James’s, not at his estate in Surrey, but on a plantation called Highfield, in the colony of Natal. The cause was vague—a fever. The estate was, by all preliminary accounts, “complex.” And Eleanor, as his sole surviving blood relation, was named executrix and principal beneficiary.

There was a second, slimmer paper folded within. The handwriting was different, a spidery, hurried scrawl.

*Eleanor,*

*If you are reading this, I have succumbed to this damned climate or my own poor judgement. Do not trust the pretty maps they sell in London. Highfield is not an estate; it is an aftermath. It is a beautiful, bitter place I carved from the veld, and it is soaked in choices I would unmake. The law says it is yours now. The truth is more complicated. Come if you dare. Or sell it sight unseen to the Company and remain in your fens. But if you come… bring a strong stomach and your father’s eyes. He always saw the rot in the wood.*

*Yours in regret,*
*Alistair*

The letter slipped from her fingers, fluttering to the worn Turkey carpet. Outside, she could hear Carter at the pump, the metallic groan and splash, the mare’s grateful snort. The fenland sky pressed down, a vast, seamless bowl of grey. Her world was one of manageable debts, of stubborn soil, of the slow turn of the seasons. It was a life of quiet desperation, yes, but it was a known quantity.

Her father had rarely spoken of his younger brother. There had been a quarrel, long ago, over money, over ambition, over the very nature of England. Her father believed in roots, in sinking deep into one patch of earth. Alistair had believed in horizons. He had gone out with a rifle and a bank loan, part of that great, confident wave of men who planted the Union Jack and called it cultivation. Her father had called it piracy with a ploughshare.

Now the pirate was dead, and he had left her his ship, adrift in a southern sea of grass.

Carter’s footsteps echoed in the flagstoned hallway. He paused at the parlour door, hat in hand. “Miss?”

Eleanor bent and retrieved the letter from the floor. The paper was cool. “There is a reply, Mr. Carter.”

She crossed to her father’s old escritoire, its wood scarred by generations of farm accounts. She took out a sheet of paper, her own pen scratching a decisive, practical counterpoint to her uncle’s haunted scrawl.

*To the Directors of the Natal Colonial Company,*

*I acknowledge receipt of notification regarding the death of Sir Alistair Chadwick and the disposition of his property, Highfield. Please forward all pertinent documents, inventories, and outstanding liens to Willowbrook. I will be arriving in Natal to assess the estate in person. I expect full cooperation from your agents on the ground.*

*Yours faithfully,*
*Eleanor Chadwick*

She sanded the note, folded it, and sealed it with plain wax. She handed it to Carter. “For your employers.”

He took it, his eyes flickering to the open solicitor’s letter on the desk. “You’re going out there, miss?”

“It appears I am.”

A faint, almost imperceptible shift crossed his features—not quite pity, not quite alarm. It was the look a man might give a lamb walking willingly towards a strange pen. “It’s a different world.”

“So I am told.” Her voice was firmer than she felt. “Thank you for your service, Carter. Your horse is watered. You’ll want to start back before the light fails.”

After he had gone, the silence of the farm settled back, deeper now, pregnant with impending absence. She walked out into the yard. The fen stretched away, a vast, green mosaic of ditch and field, horizon broken only by the occasional skeletal willow. It was a landscape that had been tamed, drained, forced into productive order over centuries. It was a finished picture.

Her uncle had written of an *aftermath*. The word, in her farming lexicon, meant the second crop of grass after the first hay harvest. A regrowth, often thinner, tougher, nourished by what had been cut down and left to decay. What had been cut down on Highfield? What was regrowing in its place?

She thought of the ledgers in the parlour, the creeping red. She thought of the neighbours’ polite, unspoken assessments, the offers for her land that were just shy of insult. She was twenty-seven. She was a woman of property, yet property was slowly consuming her. This was a life of waiting for the next blow, the next storm, the next poor harvest.

Natal was a blow already struck. A fever, a death, a bequest. It was chaos, but it was a *different* chaos. It was a horizon.

That evening, she took a lantern and climbed the narrow stairs to the attic. Trunks and memories gathered dust under the sloped eaves. She found her father’s old campaign chest, from his brief, youthful service in the cavalry. It smelled of camphor and neglect. Inside, beneath a folded uniform she could not remember him wearing, was a wooden case. She lifted it out.

The pair of Webley revolvers lay in fitted green baize, the blued steel gone dull with time but not neglect. They were heavy, solid, final in her hands. Her father had taught her to shoot on the back meadow, aiming at painted tin targets shaped like foxes. *A tool for vermin, Ellie,* he’d said, his large hands guiding hers. *Nothing more, nothing less. Respect it.* She had been fifteen, and it had felt like a secret pact, a knowledge her mother would not approve of.

She took one, along with a box of cartridges. She did not know if there were foxes in Natal. She suspected the vermin might wear a different shape.

Downstairs, she spread the solicitor’s maps on the kitchen table, weighing down the corners with mugs. Natal was a vague shape, a cartographer’s conjecture on the east coast of Africa. The interior was mostly blank, labelled “Zulu Kingdom” or simply “Unsurveyed Territory.” Highfield was marked with a small, precise star, nestled in the foothills of the Drakensberg mountains. A river curled near it, named on the map as the Umzinyathi. The Buffalo River.

She traced the route with a finger: Boston to London by coach, London to Cape Town by steamer, then… overland. Weeks of travel. An inversion of her world.

Mrs. Dalrymple from the neighbouring farm found her there at dawn, the maps still spread, the revolver case sitting beside the butter crock. The older woman took in the scene with one swift, comprehending glance.

“So the rumours are true. You’re leaving us for the Dark Continent.”

“It is not dark on the map, Agnes. It is beige.”

“Don’t be clever, child. It’s a graveyard for English bones. Your uncle’s proved that.”

“My uncle’s bones have provided me with an opportunity. Here, I am slowly drowning. There, I may at least sink or swim on my own terms.”

Mrs. Dalrymple sighed, a sound like wind through reeds. She put a basket of eggs on the table. “You have your father’s stubbornness. And his poor sense of timing. What will you do with Willowbrook?”

“Let it to the Brewsters. They’ve wanted the north pasture for years. The rent will cover the taxes, with a little left. If I don’t return in two years, they can buy it outright.”

“A tidy plan.” Agnes Dalrymple’s eyes were sharp. “And if you find more than you bargained for out there? Plantations are not fen farms, Eleanor. They run on a different kind of labour.”

The unspoken word hung in the air between them, thick as the peat smoke from the hearth. Eleanor had read the papers. She knew the debates raging in London, the pious horror, the economic defensiveness. Slavery was abolished, dead, a moral victory. And yet the sugar in her tea, the cotton in her dress, spoke of other, subtler chains.

“My uncle’s letter said it was an ‘aftermath.’ I intend to see what is growing there.”

“Aftermaths,” said the old woman, turning to go, “are often just fields of weeds and bones. But you’ll see it for yourself, I suppose. That’s the Chadwick curse. Never believing the weather report until you’re soaked to the skin.”

Alone again, Eleanor packed. Practical clothes. Good boots. The Webley and cartridges, wrapped in oilcloth. Her father’s old brass telescope. A blank journal. She left behind the porcelain shepherdesses, the watercolours of the fens, the life that felt like a dress two sizes too small.

On her last morning, she walked the boundary of Willowbrook. The ditches glittered with stagnant water. The willows whispered. It was a gentle, sodden, demanding place. It had claimed her parents. It would have claimed her, too, slowly and quietly.

She did not look back as the cart carried her to the coaching inn. She held the wooden case on her lap, feeling the weight of the metal within. She was not travelling towards an inheritance, she realised. She was travelling towards an aftermath. And she was, herself, the next crop, pushing up through the fertile, unsettling decay.

---

# Chapter 2

## **Chapter 2**

The *SS Persephone* was a iron-hulled beast that stank of hot oil, boiled mutton, and the profound, inescapable scent of human confinement. For thirty-seven days, it groaned its way south, a floating microcosm of the Empire. Eleanor’s world shrank to the dimensions of her first-class cabin—a mercifully private, panelled cell—and the teak-planked promenade deck where she took her daily constitutional, clinging to the rail as the ship carved through the swelling, indifferent Atlantic.

Her fellow passengers were a gallery of colonial types. There were officials with sun-reddened necks and opinions on native policy, their wives in impractical lace wilting in the equatorial heat; missionaries with feverish eyes and tracts; young men with adventure novels and vague plans for fortune; a few sharp-faced traders who played cards in the smoking room and spoke of ivory and gum-copal.

Eleanor kept her own counsel. She was an object of mild, polite speculation. A young woman, unchaperoned, travelling to take possession of a Natal plantation? It was irregular. The officers were punctiliously helpful. The ladies were cautiously kind, their invitations to tea laced with unasked questions. She deflected them with a rehearsed, brittle competence, speaking of “estate business” and “family duty” in a tone that brooked no further inquiry. She spent her hours reading the dense, legal documents from Pargeter & Swift, studying isiZulu phrasebooks, and writing in her journal. The entries were factual, dry: the weather, the ship’s position, the meals. She did not write of the loneliness, a cold stone in her belly, or the creeping dread that Mrs. Dalrymple had been right.

One afternoon, a week before they were due to make Cape Town, she found herself sharing a leeward corner of the deck with a man named Josiah Finch. He was a botanist, travelling to catalogue the flora of the Drakensberg foothills. He was perhaps fifty, with a kind, weathered face and fingers permanently stained with ink.

“You are bound for the Umzinyathi valley, Miss Chadwick?” he asked, after the usual pleasantries about the weather.

“I am. For a property called Highfield.”

A subtle change came over his amiable expression. He looked out at the sea, his eyes narrowing as if focusing on a distant, unwelcome sight. “Highfield. Sir Alistair’s place.”

“You knew my uncle?”

“I met him once, at a gathering in Pietermaritzburg. Two years ago. A forceful man. Certain of his… vision.” Finch chose his words like a man picking his way through a thorny thicket. “His coffee was the talk of the colony, for a season. A bold experiment, planting it at that altitude.”

“Was?”

Finch sighed. “The talk turned, Miss Chadwick. The yields were legendary, then they fell off a cliff. Blight, some said. Poor management, whispered others. Labour troubles, said the rest. The usual colonial trilogy.” He glanced at her, his gaze appraising. “You go to manage it yourself?”

“To assess it. As executrix.”

“A formidable task.” He paused. “The land there… it is not like Lincolnshire. It is not even like the Cape. It is ancient. It has a memory. Men like your uncle, they see a blank canvas. They slash, they burn, they plant. They believe the story they are writing is the only one. But the land has older stories, written in stone and river. And it has its own authors.”

His words echoed her uncle’s letter: *an aftermath*. “You speak as if the land is sentient, Mr. Finch.”

“Not sentient. Patient.” He tapped the ship’s rail. “This iron ship, it is a triumph of will over nature. We cross this ocean because we insist upon it. In Zululand, in those foothills, the will is not so clearly on one side. The land absorbs ambition. It weathers it. Sometimes, it simply waits for the ambitious man to leave, or to die, and then it begins to reclaim its own. Your uncle was a man of immense will. I suspect the land is now waiting for you.”

The conversation left her unsettled. That night, in her bunk, the ship’s screws thrumming beneath her, she dreamed not of Africa, but of the fens. She was digging in the kitchen garden, but the soil gave way to a dry, red sand that sucked at her hands. She pulled, and from the earth came not a carrot, but a tarnished brass button, then a broken clay pipe, then a human tooth, smooth and yellow. She woke with a start, her heart pounding, to the sound of the ship’s bell.

Cape Town was a shock of colour and noise and blinding light. Table Mountain loomed, a stark, geological authority over the bustling, whitewashed town. The air smelled of dust, dung, exotic spices, and the sea. Here, the hierarchies were starker, painted in broader strokes. Eleanor felt her Englishness like a suit of clothes, both a protection and a marker. She transferred to a smaller, grimier coastal steamer for the journey east to Durban.

Durban was raw. Where Cape Town had Dutch gables and a sense of settled history, Durban was a frontier scramble of mud streets, corrugated iron roofs, and a frantic, commercial energy. The harbour was thick with ships loading wool, sugar, and timber. The air hummed with insects and a dozen different languages. Zulu rickshaw pullers, magnificent in headdresses of ox horns and beads, trotted past Indian merchants in spotless white dhotis. Red-coated British soldiers sweltered next to Boer farmers in broad-brimmed hats. It was a chaotic symphony of possession and displacement.

She was met on the dock by a man from the Natal Colonial Company’s office, a clerk named Dobbs with a damp handshake and a sheaf of paperwork.

“Miss Chadwick! A warm welcome, a warm welcome indeed. We have your transport arranged. A wagon leaves for Pietermaritzburg tomorrow at first light. From there, you can secure a guide to Highfield. It’s a further two days’ ride into the foothills.” He peered at her. “You’ll be wanting to stay at the Royal Hotel tonight. It’s the only proper accommodation for a lady.”

“Thank you, Mr. Dobbs. Before that, I would like to see the Company’s files on Highfield. The current inventory. The labour rolls.”

Dobbs’s smile tightened. “Ah. Yes. Of course. The office is just here. Though I must warn you, communications from the interior have been… sporadic since Sir Alistair’s passing. The manager he left in charge, a Mr. Shaw, sends reports when he can.”

The office was a dark, wooden room, fans stirring the thick air. The file on Highfield was surprisingly thin. The inventory listed a main house, several outbuildings, a coffee processing shed, 200 acres under cultivation (coffee, a trial plot of tea), and basic farming implements. The labour roll was a single page, a list of twenty names, each followed by a tribal designation and a monthly wage. The wages were pitifully low, even to Eleanor’s eyes. The last entry, for a man listed as “Mkize, foreman,” was annotated in pencil: *Gone.*

“What does ‘gone’ mean, Mr. Dobbs?”

“Deserted, miss. Common enough. They get notions, wander off back to their kraals. Mr. Shaw’s last letter said several of the hands left after Sir Alistair died. The native mind is superstitious. A death on the property… unsettles them.”

Something about the way he said it, the easy, dismissive categorization, set her teeth on edge. It was the tone her uncle might have used. “And the coffee? The yield reports have stopped.”

“The last shipment was eighteen months ago, miss. Small, but of excellent quality. Nothing since. Mr. Shaw reported a blight. Then the labour troubles. It is, I am afraid, a dormant concern. The Company would, of course, be prepared to make a fair offer for the title. Save you the arduous journey for no purpose.”

So that was it. The squeeze. Let the woman venture into the dangerous interior for a rotting estate, or sell it cheaply now to the comfortable men in town. She felt a familiar, stubborn anger warm her blood.

“Thank you for the offer, Mr. Dobbs. I will assess the purpose for myself.”

That night, in her room at the Royal Hotel, she laid out the Webley on the bedspread. She cleaned and loaded it with methodical care, the ritual calming her nerves. The metal was cool, the mechanisms smooth with oil. *A tool for vermin.* She looked at her reflection in the mottled mirror. A pale, serious face looked back, framed by hair already escaping its pins in the humidity. She did not look like a woman who could reclaim an aftermath. She looked like a librarian who had taken a wrong turn.

But in her eyes, she saw a glint of her father’s stubbornness. And perhaps, she feared, a shadow of her uncle’s reckless will.

The wagon to Pietermaritzburg was a bone-jarring ordeal along a track that was little more than a rutted scar through the rolling green hills of Natal. The landscape was lush, beautiful, and utterly alien. Strange, flat-topped acacia trees dotted the hillsides. Birds of impossible colour flashed through the thickets. The sun was a physical weight.

In Maritzburg, a dusty, administrative town trying hard to mimic an English county seat, she hired a guide. His name was Thomas Ndlovu. He was a Zulu Christian, dressed in a worn but clean European suit, speaking careful, formal English. He had worked as a translator for the colonial magistrates.

“Highfield is not a good place now, *inkosazana*,” he said, when she stated her destination. He would not meet her eyes.

“Why do you say that?”

“The *inkosi*, Sir Alistair, he was a hard tree. When a hard tree falls, the ground shakes. Things are… unsettled. Mr. Shaw is not a tree. He is a shouting man.”

“I need to see it. Will you take me?”

He was silent for a long time, his gaze on the distant, purple haze of the mountains. “I will take you. For three pounds. And you listen, *inkosazana*. You listen to the land. It is speaking there.”

They left at dawn on horseback, with a pack mule carrying her trunk. As they climbed out of the town’s valley, the cultivated fields gave way to wilder country. The air grew cooler. The grass was tall and golden. They forded rivers running clear over stony beds. Thomas pointed out landmarks with his chin: “That hill, the Zulus call it *Isivivane*, the gathering of stones. Bad spirits there.” Or, “This valley, many battles. The earth remembers blood.”

His words, like Finch’s, spoke of a world layered with meaning, where every rock and stream had a history, a name, a presence. It was the antithesis of the fens, which had been drained of mystery and story centuries ago, reduced to productive, anonymous silt.

On the second day, they turned onto a narrower track. The land changed again. Steeper hills, studded with giant, grey boulders. The vegetation was lusher, darker. And then, rounding a bend, Thomas reined in his horse.

“Highfield, *inkosazana*.”

It was not what she had expected. She had envisioned a grand, white-pillared house, fields in neat rows. Instead, she saw a low, sprawling homestead of local stone and thatch, crouching on a rise like a brooding animal. To its left, terraced into the hillside, were the coffee fields. But they were not the vibrant green of cultivation. They were a sickly, mottled brown and grey, the plants twisted and sparse, strangled by a creeping, virulent-looking vine. The blight was not an abstraction; it was a visible, choking plague.

Beyond the fields, the beautiful, unforgiving landscape of the Umzinyathi valley unfolded—rolling green hills, distant blue mountains, a silver thread of river. It was breathtaking. And the plantation, this scar upon it, was clearly dying.

But it was the silence that struck her most. No sound of labourers, no axes, no voices. Only the sigh of the wind through the sick coffee bushes and the distant cry of a hawk.

Then, a door slammed at the house. A white man emerged onto the veranda, shading his eyes. He was tall, stooped, dressed in stained khaki. He carried a rifle loosely in the crook of his arm. He stared at them, not moving to welcome them, just watching their approach.

This was the aftermath. Not a field after harvest, but a ruin in the making. And she had just ridden into the centre of it.

---

# Chapter 3

## **Chapter 3**

The man on the veranda was rawboned and unshaven, his skin the colour of old saddle leather, stretched tight over a frame that seemed to be consuming itself. He didn’t lower the rifle as Eleanor and Thomas approached, though he did shift it to lean against a veranda post. His eyes, a watery blue, tracked them with the flat assessment of a man used to judging threats.

“You’d be Chadwick’s niece,” he called out, his voice a rasp. It wasn’t a question.

“I am Eleanor Chadwick. And you are Mr. Shaw?”

“Aye. Manager. Such as there is to manage.” He spat a stream of tobacco juice into a potted aloe that was brown and dying. “Wasn’t told to expect you. Mail’s unreliable.”

“The Company in Durban knew of my arrival.”

“The Company in Durban knows shit from shinola about this place.” He finally stepped down from the veranda, his movements stiff. He ignored Thomas completely. “Well, you’re here. Come and see your kingdom.”

He led her on a grim tour, his commentary a bitter monologue. The coffee fields were a total loss—“rust and scale and God knows what else.” The processing shed was empty, the machinery seized with red rust. The labourers’ quarters, a row of mud-and-wattle huts, were deserted, doors hanging open on darkness. Only a few chickens scratched in the dust.

“Where are the workers?” Eleanor asked, her voice sounding too loud in the pervasive quiet.

“Gone. Cleared out after the *Inkosi* died. Superstitious fools. Mkize, the foreman, he was the first. Poisoned the rest against the place. Said the land was cursed for the blood on it.” Shaw shot her a sideways glance. “Your uncle wasn’t one for half-measures. Had to clear the land when he first came. There were… disputes. With the locals who thought they still owned it. Mkize’s brother was killed in one. Never forgot it.”

The information landed like a physical blow. Her uncle’s “vision” had a foundation she hadn’t considered. *Soaked in choices I would unmake.* She saw the beautiful valley with new eyes: every rock, every fold in the land, could be a grave, a memory of violence.

“And the house?” she asked, turning back towards the homestead.

“Holding together. Just.”

The interior was a museum of decayed ambition. The rooms were large, with high ceilings and good bones, but they were sparsely, poorly furnished. Dust sheets covered shapes that might have been furniture. A grand piano in the drawing room stood like a tomb, its lid closed, keys yellowed. Books were piled in corners, swollen with damp. The air smelled of mildew, wood smoke, and loneliness.

Shaw had clearly been living in only two rooms: a cluttered study, where ledgers lay open next to empty brandy bottles, and a bedroom with an unmade camp bed. He made no apology for the state of things.

“I’ve kept the roof from falling in. That’s what he paid me for. To hold it. Not to work miracles.”

“What of the accounts? The revenue?”

He barked a laugh, a dry, cracking sound. “Revenue? Last revenue was from selling the last of the stored beans two years back. Since then, it’s been outgoings. My wage—which is six months in arrears, miss—taxes to the Company, supplies from Maritzburg. Your uncle had capital, but it’s bled dry. The Company holds a lien on the next two crops.” He gestured out the window at the dead fields. “Good luck with that.”

Eleanor felt the walls of the study closing in. The ledgers, the red ink—it was Willowbrook all over again, but magnified, set in a harder land with a darker history. She had crossed an ocean for another slow death.

“I will need to see all the financial records, Mr. Shaw. And the title deeds. Tonight.”

He shrugged, a gesture of supreme indifference. “Suit yourself. They’re in that desk. I’ll have the boy bring your trunk to a room. Such as it is.”

“The boy?”

“Only one who stayed. Simple in the head. Useful for chores.”

The “boy” was a young Zulu man, perhaps eighteen, who appeared silently at the kitchen door. He was tall and powerfully built, but one side of his face was slack, his eye drooping, and he moved with a careful, deliberate slowness. His name, Shaw said, was Bheki. He wouldn’t meet Eleanor’s gaze, but he hefted her trunk onto his shoulder with ease and carried it to a room at the end of the corridor.

The room was clean, at least. The sheets on the iron bed were patched but smelled of sun and air. A gecko clung to the whitewashed wall, motionless. From her window, she had a panoramic view of the dying plantation and the living, indifferent valley beyond.

Thomas helped her unpack a few essentials. His face was grave. “This is a sad place, *inkosazana*. The *umoya*… the spirit… is sick here.”

“What do I do, Thomas?”

He was silent for a long time. “You asked me to bring you. I have brought you. My work is done. I will leave at first light.”

“You won’t stay? I could hire you.”

He shook his head, a firm, final movement. “This is not my path. But I will give you a word. Find Mkize. He is not at his kraal. He is in the hills. He knows this land. He knows what happened. He hates this place, but he loves this land. It is a difference. You must understand the difference.” He paused at the door. “And do not trust the shouting man. A shouting man is an empty gourd.”

That evening, Eleanor ate a silent, miserable meal of tough stew with Shaw in the cavernous dining room. The only sounds were the scrape of cutlery and the buzz of insects against the lamp glass. Afterward, she retreated to the study.

The desk was a chaos of papers. She lit more lamps and began to sort. The title deed was clear: Highfield, 5000 acres, granted to Sir Alistair Chadwick by the Natal Colonial Company, “in perpetuity.” The phrase seemed a cruel joke. The financial records were a disaster, a trail of extravagant purchases—machinery from England, expensive seed, a grand piano—followed by a long, slow taper into nothing. Shaw’s own reports were terse, increasingly desperate, then resigned.

Buried at the bottom of a drawer, she found a different sort of document. It was a map, hand-drawn in her uncle’s precise draughtsman’s hand. It showed Highfield, but with details not on the Company map. A waterfall on the northern boundary, marked “Good Power.” A stand of forest labelled “Sacred Grove – Avoid.” And, near a koppie to the east, a small, careful “X” with the notation: *Site of ’68.*

’68. The year of the great border clashes between the British and the Zulus. The year, according to Shaw, that Mkize’s brother had died.

She was studying the map when a soft sound made her look up. Bheki stood in the doorway, holding a chipped porcelain cup of tea. He placed it carefully on the edge of the desk, his movements slow and deliberate.

“Thank you, Bheki,” she said.

He didn’t acknowledge her words, but he didn’t leave. He stood, his eyes fixed on the hand-drawn map on the desk. Then, with a finger that trembled slightly, he reached out and touched the “X” near the koppie. He looked at her, and for a fleeting second, the blankness in his drooping eye was replaced by a profound, unmistakable grief. Then it was gone, shuttered away. He turned and shuffled out.

Eleanor’s heart hammered against her ribs. The simple boy understood the map. He knew what the “X” meant. And in that moment of connection, she felt the first, fragile thread of understanding—this place was not just an agricultural failure. It was a wound.

She spent a sleepless night, the sounds of the African night—the whoop of hyenas, the chirring of insects—alien and menacing. At first light, she heard Thomas’s horse leave. She was truly alone now.

She rose and dressed, strapping the Webley’s holster to her waist beneath her skirt. It was an unfamiliar, comforting weight. She found Shaw on the veranda, drinking coffee and staring at the dead fields as if his gaze could resurrect them.

“Mr. Shaw, I’m going to walk the boundary. To see the full extent.”

He shrugged. “Watch for snakes. And don’t go poking around the eastern koppie. Ground’s unstable there.”

His warning only fixed her destination more firmly in her mind.

She walked for hours, following the rough stone cairns that marked the property line. The beauty of the land was a constant, aching counterpoint to the ruin of the plantation. She saw antelope in the distance, and a magnificent secretary bird stalked through the long grass. The “Good Power” waterfall was a dazzling ribbon of white, crashing into a crystal pool. It felt alive, powerful, clean.

By mid-afternoon, she approached the eastern koppie, a small, rocky hill covered in thorn trees. The ground did not feel unstable. It felt still, watchful. Near its base, partly hidden by a fallen aloe, she saw it: not an “X” on a map, but a reality.

A low, unmarked mound of stones, roughly piled. Not a natural formation. A grave. And beside it, driven into the earth, was a simple, wooden cross, so weathered it was almost grey. No name. Just a cross.

This was it. The blood on the land. The choice her uncle would unmake. The reason Mkize hated this place. The source of Bheki’s grief.

As she stood there, the immensity of her task crashed down upon her. She was not just an executrix of an estate. She was the inheritor of a sin. She could not simply tally assets and liabilities. She was standing in the aftermath, and the second growth—if there was to be one—would have to push through this.

A movement on the koppie above caught her eye. A man stood among the rocks, silhouetted against the sky. He was tall, wearing a blanket over one shoulder. He was not Bheki, and he was not Shaw. He stood perfectly still, watching her.

He made no move to approach. He simply observed, a sentinel on the high ground. This, she knew with a cold certainty, was Mkize. He had been waiting. Not for her, perhaps, but for someone to come to this spot. To see.

She did not wave, did not call out. She met his gaze across the distance, the grave between them. Then, slowly, she turned and began the long walk back to the dead house, feeling his eyes on her back every step of the way.

The first complication was not financial. It was moral. And it was standing on a hill, waiting to see what the *inkosazana*, the chief’s daughter, would do next.



---

# Chapter 4

## **Chapter 4**

The dead house was not empty. Shaw was there, perched on the edge of the dusty stoep, whittling a stick with a bone-handled knife. The long shavings curled and fell like pale petals onto the boards. He did not look up as Eleanor approached, her boots heavy with the red dust of the koppie. He finished his cut, blew on the wood, and then his eyes lifted. They took her in, from her wind-tangled hair to the grave-dust on her hem.

“Found it, then,” he said. It was not a question.

She climbed the steps, her body a column of weariness. “You knew it was there.”

“A man knows the bones of his own land.” He sheathed the knife. “Sit. You look like you’ve seen a ghost.”

“I saw a man on the hill.”

Shaw’s whittling stilled for a fraction of a second. “Mkize.”

“He was watching.”

“He watches everything. The land is his church. That grave is its altar.” Shaw tossed the stick into the yard. “You’ve had your tour. The pretty waterfall, the good grass. And the bad blood. Now you know the shape of it.”

Eleanor lowered herself into the other chair, its cane creaking a protest. The immensity she had felt at the grave was now a cold, solid weight in her chest. “Who is in it?”

“Does it matter?” Shaw’s voice was flat. “A name won’t change the fact of it. It’s a Xhosa burial. Before your uncle’s time, likely. But it’s on his deed. And when he found it, he didn’t move it. He just… worked around it. Like a tree stump too deep to dig out. Drove Mkize mad. A sacred place, turned into a paddock corner.”

“And my uncle’s choice? To unmake it?”

Shaw leaned back, the chair groaning. “His grand idea. Restitution. Give the koppie, and a buffer of land around it, back. Formally. Legally. Cut it out of the title like a cancer. Let it go fallow. Let Mkize’s people tend it. A clean wound, he thought. Then the rest of Thornveld could be… purified. A fresh start.”

“But it’s not a clean wound,” Eleanor said, the words tasting of the iron-red dust. “It’s a festering one. And I am the surgeon who does not know how to cut.”

“Now you’re learning,” Shaw said. There was no sympathy in it, only a grim acknowledgment. “Your first lesson: nothing here is simple. A gift can be an insult. A kindness, a provocation. Mkize doesn’t want a piece of paper from a dead white man. He wants the whole land weeping for what’s been done to it. He might settle for the koppie, but it’ll be a starting point, not an end.”

Eleanor looked out over the land, the long shadows of late afternoon stretching across the veld like grasping fingers. “And Bheki?”

“Bheki is a practical man. He sees a farm that could feed families. He sees the ghost in the ground, too, but he thinks a living, working land is a better memorial than a fallow shrine. He and Mkize… they argue. Not with words, often. With silence. With where they choose to stand.”

She understood then. She was not just an inheritor of sin. She was a pivot point in an old, silent argument. “What do you see, Mr. Shaw?”

He was quiet for a long time. “I see a farm,” he said finally. “Just a farm. Land is land. It bears what you plant in it. Corn, or guilt, or stones. It doesn’t care. Only the people care. And people are the most exhausting crop of all.”

He stood, his joints cracking. “We’ll need supplies. The storehouse is ghosts and cobwebs. I’ll ride to the trading post at first light. You should come. See the nearest approximation of civilization this side of the escarpment. Meet the vultures.”

“Vultures?”

“The ones who circle,” he said, and went inside.

***
The trading post was a day’s ride east, a cluster of tin-roofed buildings clinging to the crossroads like lichen to a rock. The air here smelled different—less of grass and sun, more of dust, ox-dung, and the faint, metallic tang of machinery. A rusted windmill creaked in the hot breeze. Eleanor felt exposed riding in beside Shaw, the eyes of men lounging on verandas following her like physical things, sticky and assessing.

Shaw led the way to a long, low building with a sign that read **VAN ROOYEN’S PROVISIONS & MERCANTILE**. Inside, the light was dim and thick with the smell of burlap, coffee, and paraffin. A broad man with a beard the colour of rust stood behind a counter, weighing nails on a brass scale. He looked up, and his face arranged itself into a smile that did not touch his eyes.

“Shaw. Heard you had new management.” His gaze slid to Eleanor. “Miss Chadwick. A sorrow about your uncle. A difficult situation you find yourself in.”

His tone was a veneer of civility over a deep, unspoken curiosity. Eleanor felt like a specimen. “Mr. van Rooyen. We require supplies. A basic list.”

“Of course, of course.” He took the list Shaw handed over, his eyes scanning it. “Ambitious. Planning to stay a while, then? To… resolve the estate?”

“To assess it,” Eleanor said, her voice cooler than she felt.

“A complex assessment.” Van Rooyen leaned on the counter, lowering his voice conspiratorially. “You must beware the sentimentalities, Miss Chadwick. The natives have a great capacity for weaving stories around unproductive land. It can trap a well-meaning person. Your uncle, God rest him, was a dreamer. Land is a commodity. Its value is in what it can yield. Not in what stories are buried in it.”

The words were smooth, reasonable. The voice of the ledger, the bottom line. It was a voice she understood, and for a moment, it was a relief. It was the voice of London, of solicitors’ offices. But here, with the dust of the unmarked grave still on her boots, it sounded hollow, a bell ringing in a vacuum.

“Thank you for the advice,” she said, noncommittal.

As van Rooyen began assembling their order, a younger man entered from a back room. He had the same rust-coloured hair but none of the father’s practised geniality. His eyes were sharp, hungry. He stared at Eleanor with a frankness that made her skin prickle.

“Father. The shipment from Port Elizabeth is delayed. Damned railways.” His voice was a nasal whine. He didn’t acknowledge Shaw.

“This is my son, Adriaan,” van Rooyen said. “Adriaan, Miss Chadwick of Thornveld.”

Adriaan’s smile was a thin slash. “Thornveld. A pretty name for a troublesome patch. We heard you had a spot of bother with your labour. Mkize’s lot clearing off.”

News travelled fast on the dry wind. “The situation is stable,” Eleanor lied.

“Stable?” Adriaan gave a short laugh. “With that old witch-doctor sitting on the koppie? He’s a blight. Scares off proper workers, stirs up nonsense. If you’re smart, you’ll sell. Cut your losses. There are men here,” he said, his gaze lingering on her, “who would make you a fair offer. For the *viable* portions, of course. The koppie’s just rock and superstition.”

The proposition hung in the oily air. It was the first trial, not of the veld, but of the world of men who sought to carve it up. The easy way out. Cash for the clean parts, abandonment of the infected wound. She felt Shaw’s silent presence beside her, a watchful stillness.

“The estate is not for sale,” Eleanor said, the words coming out firmer than she expected.

Adriaan’s eyes hardened. “Sentiment is a luxury, miss. One this district can ill afford. Think on it. The offer won’t improve with time.” He turned and disappeared into the back.

Van Rooyen sighed, a performance of paternal exasperation. “Forgive my son. He is blunt, but not incorrect. The district watches Thornveld. Its… resolution… will set a tone. A precedent.” He finished packing their supplies into sacks. “You will find, Miss Chadwick, that here, you are never merely managing a farm. You are taking a side.”

They loaded the sacks in silence. As Shaw tightened the straps on the packhorse, he spoke low. “You see? The vultures. They’ve had their eye on Thornveld for years. Your uncle wouldn’t sell. Now they’re waiting to see if the niece is softer. If the trouble with Mkize will soften you further.”

“They want the land without the koppie.”
“They want the land, full stop. The koppie they’d dynamite for quarry stone, or give to Mkize as a sop to quiet him. They don’t see a grave. They see an obstacle to grazing.” He checked his rifle in its scabbard. “You’ve met the first kind of enemy. The one with a ledger and a smile. They’re more dangerous than a leopard, because they think they’re civilized.”

***
The ride back was slower, burdened by the supplies. The sun was a furious orange ball sinking toward the black line of the escarpment. Eleanor’s mind churned with the van Rooyens’ words, with the image of Mkize on the hill, with the cold, nameless grave.

They were an hour from Thornveld when Shaw reined in sharply, his hand raised. He was staring at a thicket of thorn trees off the track’s left side. The air was still. Too still. The usual insect hum had ceased.

“What is it?” Eleanor whispered.

Before he could answer, a figure stepped from the dappled shadow of the trees. It was Bheki. But not the composed, reserved Bheki of the farmyard. His shirt was torn at the shoulder. A fresh, bloody weal marked his cheek. In his hand, he held a *knobkierrie*, a fighting stick, its heavy head dark and stained.

“Bheki.” Shaw’s voice was a low warning.

“They are gone,” Bheki said, his voice hollow with a fury so deep it sounded like grief. “The last of them. The boys who were mending the fence line. Mkize came down from the koppie. He spoke to them. Now they are gone.”

Eleanor’s stomach clenched. “All of them?”

“All. Only I remain. He would not speak to me. He only looked.” Bheki’s knuckles were white on the stick. “He has called them all to the high ground. He says the *inkosazana* has seen the blood and has done nothing. He says the time for waiting is over.”

This was the direct encounter. Not with Mkize himself, but with his will, enacted. The farm was now truly a dead place, stripped of its lifeblood. The obstacle was no longer just moral; it was physical, absolute.

“Where are they?” Shaw asked, his eyes scanning the surrounding veld.

“The koppie. They are making camp. They will not work. They will watch.” Bheki finally looked at Eleanor. “You went to the grave. You saw him. What did you do?”

“Nothing,” Eleanor said, the word aching with inadequacy. “I only saw.”

“To see and do nothing is a choice,” Bheki said. “He believes you have chosen the farm. The dirt. The dead man’s paper.”

“I have chosen nothing yet!”
“Here, silence is a answer,” Shaw said grimly. “We’re sitting ducks on this track. To the farm. Now.”

They urged the horses into a canter, Bheki loping alongside with an easy, ground-eating stride. Eleanor felt the exposure between her shoulder blades, expecting at any moment a thrown spear, a cry, a rush of men from the rocks. But only the watching silence followed them, pressed in on them, until the dead house’s tin roof glinted in the last of the light.

They secured the animals in the barn. Shaw barred the farmhouse door, a gesture that felt futile against the vast, encircling night. In the kitchen, Bheki accepted a glass of water, his anger settling into a smouldering resignation.

“He will not attack the house,” Bheki said. “Not yet. This is not war. It is a… demonstration. A showing of strength. The land is empty without its people. He shows you its true face.”

“And what is your place in this, Bheki?” Eleanor asked, sitting across from him. “You are caught between.”

“I am a man of this land,” he said. “Mkize is my uncle. His truth is a true truth. The blood is real. The disrespect is real.” He set the glass down carefully. “But my father, who is dead, worked for your uncle. He taught me the soil, the cattle, the seasons. This is also a true truth. Mkize says one truth must erase the other. I do not know if that is so.”

In his conflict, Eleanor saw the mirror of her own. Here was her potential ally, solidified not by shared purpose, but by shared paralysis. They were both stranded in the no-man’s-land between two opposing certainties.

“We cannot run the farm with two people,” Shaw stated the obvious. “And we can’t bring in new labour with Mkize camped on the koppie. He’d scare them off, or worse.”

“Then the problem is the koppie,” Eleanor said, the germ of a desperate plan forming. “My uncle’s wish was to give it back. Formally. We do that. We draw up the papers. We sign it over to Mkize, or to his clan. We make the gesture. That was the plan.”

Bheki shook his head slowly. “It is not a gesture he wants from you. You are the inheritor. The sin is in your hands. A paper from you is… dirty. It is the same hand that took, now offering a piece back. He may refuse it. Spit on it.”

“Then what does he want?” Eleanor’s voice rose in frustration. “If not the land back, if not an apology from me, what?”

Bheki was silent for a long moment. “He wants the land to be whole again. In his mind, it cannot be whole while any white hand holds any part of it. But he is not a fool. He knows he cannot have all. So he wants… a sacrifice. Not of land. Of pride. Of history. He wants you to feel the weight of the grave until you cannot bear it, and you give not from charity, but from exhaustion. From defeat.”

Shaw let out a long breath. “So we’re in a siege. A siege of conscience. And our supplies,” he nodded to the sacks they’d brought in, “won’t last forever.”

“Then we break the siege,” Eleanor said. “We go to him. Not with papers. With… a question.”

“A question,” Shaw repeated, scepticism dripping from the word.

“We ask him what would make the land clean. Not in law. In his truth. We listen. It may be something we cannot give. It may be nothing. But we will have moved. Silence is our enemy now.”

Bheki looked at her, a flicker of something like respect in his weary eyes. “It is a risk. He may see it as weakness. He may demand everything.”

“He already has everything but this house and the dust,” Eleanor said. “We have nothing to lose but our own confusion.”

It was a plan born of exhaustion and dwindling options. A plan to walk into the heart of the obstacle. They agreed Bheki would go at first light, alone, to request a parley. A meeting at the grave. Neutral ground.

***
Bheki left in the cool, grey dawn. Eleanor and Shaw waited on the stoep, the coffee in their cups growing cold. The farmyard was a theatre of absence. No lowing of cattle, no calls of men, only the mocking chirp of sparrows.

It was nearly noon when Bheki returned. His face was unreadable.
“He will meet,” Bheki said. “At the grave. Two hours before sunset. You may bring the *umlungu*,” he meant Shaw, “for your safety. But only you,” his eyes fixed on Eleanor, “may speak. He will only hear the *inkosazana*.”

It was a concession, but a brittle one. Eleanor spent the afternoon preparing nothing. There were no papers to gather, no arguments to marshal. She simply had to be present, to listen. It felt like the most difficult task she had ever faced.

As the sun began its descent, painting the sky in washes of ochre and violet, they walked. Shaw carried his rifle, but held it loosely, pointed at the earth. Bheki led. The path to the eastern koppie felt longer, more portentous, this time.

He was already there.

Mkize stood beside the piled stones, no longer a silhouette on the hill but a man of flesh and presence. He was older than she had guessed, his face a map of deep lines, his hair grizzled. The blanket over his shoulder was a deep, rich indigo. He held a tall staff, its wood dark with age and handling. His eyes were the colour of the earth at twilight, and they held no welcome, only a profound, patient assessment.

Eleanor stopped a dozen paces from the grave. Shaw hung back further. Bheki stood midway between, a bridge that could not hold.

She waited. The rule was clear: she must speak first. The weight of the silence was immense, a physical pressure on her lungs.

“Sawubona, Mkize,” she said, using the greeting Bheki had taught her.

He inclined his head, a minute motion. His gaze never left her face.

“I have seen the grave,” she continued, her voice sounding too loud in the sacred quiet. “I do not know the name of the person who rests here. But I see that it is sacred. That it has been… dishonoured.”

Mkize spoke. His voice was low, gravelly, like stones grinding in a deep river. “Dishonour is a weak word. It is a wound that does not close. The plough has bitten the edges. The cattle have trampled the earth. The spirit is restless. It makes the land sick. It makes the people who live on it sick.”

“My uncle wished to give this place back. To make it right.”
“A man cannot give back what he stole. He can only stop stealing. He is dead. The stealing is not.” Mkize’s staff tapped gently on the earth beside the grave. “You stand in his place. What do you stop?”

Eleanor swallowed. “I wish to stop the sickness.”
“How?”
“By listening. What would heal this place? What would let the spirit rest?”

A long pause. A secretary bird cried, high and lonely, in the distance.
“The stone is piled. The cross is foreign,” Mkize said. “But the heart is missing.”

“The heart?”
“The *isivivane*,” he said, gesturing to the cairn. “It is a prayer of stones. Each stone a memory, a respect. But the first stone, the heart-stone, was taken. A white man, long ago, took it. A smooth, black river stone from the pool below the waterfall. He took it for a paperweight. A curiosity.” Mkize’s eyes burned with a cold fire. “The prayer is broken. You cannot mend a broken prayer with a paper. You must return the heart.”

It was a demand both utterly specific and impossibly vast. A single stone, lost to time, likely sitting on some forgotten desk in England, or lost in the clutter of a dead man’s life.

“I do not have this stone,” Eleanor said.
“Then you have nothing,” Mkize said, and there was a finality in it. “The land will remain sick. My people will not touch it. They will watch it sicken until even you must abandon it. Then, perhaps, the wild will heal it in time.”

It was a sentence of slow death for Thornveld. Eleanor felt a desperate clarity. The legalities, the deeds, the van Rooyens and their offers—they were irrelevant. The true stronghold was this man’s conviction, and its key was a missing stone.

“If I could find it,” she said, the words a leap into darkness. “If I could return it. Would that be enough? Would the prayer be mended?”

Mkize studied her for an eternity. “It would be a beginning,” he conceded. “A true gesture. Not of law, but of memory. Then we could speak of land, of stones, of what is to be done.”

It was a thread of hope, gossamer-thin. “I will try,” she said.
He nodded once, then turned his back, looking up at the koppie where the shapes of his people could be seen among the rocks. The audience was over.

They walked back to the dead house in a silence thicker than before. The task was now defined, but no less impossible. Find a single, specific stone, somewhere in the world.

***
That night, Eleanor ransacked her uncle’s study. She pulled out every drawer, emptied every box, sifted through the detritus of a colonial life—faded receipts, blunt pencils, tobacco tins, hunting licenses. She found maps, journals, ledgers. But no smooth, black river stone.

Exhausted, near despair, she slumped into his leather chair. Her eyes fell on the one thing she had not touched: the large, iron-banded chest that had come with her from England, containing the personal effects shipped after her uncle’s death. She had not had the heart to open it.

Now, with a feverish urgency, she dragged it to the centre of the room and threw back the lid. It smelled of cedar and mothballs and the faint, salty damp of the sea voyage. On top lay clothes, a battered telescope, a pair of field glasses. Beneath those, books. And beneath the books, wrapped in oilcloth, a bundle of letters and a small, locked rosewood box.

The key was on her uncle’s keyring. Her hands shook as she fitted it into the lock. The lid opened with a sigh.

Inside, nestled on a bed of faded velvet, were trinkets: a broken pocket watch, a medal of no distinction, a dried and pressed flower. And there, to one side, was a stone. Smooth, oval, black as a night without stars, polished by water and time. It was cool and heavy in her palm. A perfect paperweight.

Her heart hammered against her ribs. This was it. The heart of the cairn. The missing piece. A wave of dizzying relief washed over her, followed immediately by a chilling doubt. It was too easy. Too convenient. She had the answer, the key to the siege, in her hand.

She carried it to the window, holding it up in the lamplight. It was just a stone. And yet it was everything. She would return it at first light. It was a victory, a path forward.

A floorboard creaked behind her. She turned, clutching the stone.
Bheki stood in the doorway. His eyes went from her face to the stone in her hand. His expression, usually so guarded, shifted through surprise, recognition, and then something that looked like profound sorrow.

“You found it,” he said, his voice flat.
“In my uncle’s things. It was here all along. This is it, isn’t it?”
Bheki did not answer directly. He stepped into the room, his movements weary. “Mkize did not tell you the whole truth of the heart-stone.”

“What do you mean?”
“The white man who took it… it was not a stranger. It was your uncle’s father. Your grandfather. He took it the day the first surveyors came. He kept it as a trophy of his conquest.” Bheki’s eyes were dark pools of pity. “Mkize did not tell you, because he wanted to see if you would find it. And if you did… what you would do with it. The test was not in the finding, *Inkosazana*. The test is now.”

Eleanor’s hand closed around the stone, its smoothness now feeling slick, treacherous. “What do you mean? I will return it. As I said.”

“To return it is to acknowledge the theft. To acknowledge the thief. Not just a white man. Your blood.” Bheki took a step closer. “And if you do that, publicly, at the grave… you will break the last thread of legitimacy you have here. The van Rooyens, the other farmers—they will say you have gone native. That you have sided with superstition against property. They will use it to isolate you, to pressure you further. They may even call your competence into question with the authorities. It is not just a stone. It is your standing. Your last shred of power.”

The cold certainty of his words seeped into her. He was not threatening her. He was explaining the mechanism of the trap. Mkize’s true trial. He had forced her to find the evidence of her own family’s sin. And now he waited to see if she would confess it, knowing the colonial world would punish her for that honesty.

“You knew this,” Eleanor whispered. “You knew what finding it would mean.”
“I suspected,” Bheki admitted. “I did not know if it still existed. But I know Mkize. His justice is a mirror. It shows you your own face.”

“And you?” Eleanor asked, the betrayal sharp in her throat. “Whose side are you on in this test?”

Bheki looked at the stone in her hand, then at the empty farmyard through the window, a world of broken loyalties. “I am on the side of the land,” he said softly. “And the land is bleeding. I brought you the message for the meeting. I did not… I did not know you would search tonight.”

But he had known the nature of the test. He had let her walk into it blind. The ally she had begun to trust had withheld the crucial truth. The solidification of their partnership was now riddled with the fault lines of his own divided heart.

She was alone. With a stone in her hand that was no longer a key, but a weapon—one that could break the siege, or break her.

Outside, the vast African night pressed against the windows, alive with the cries of unseen creatures. On the eastern koppie, the cooking fires of Mkize’s people winked like cold, watchful stars. She held the stolen heart of the land, and every possible path forward seemed to lead into a different darkness. The moral complication had become a personal one. The ground beneath her, which had finally felt still, now yawned open, unstable and hungry, waiting to swallow her choice whole.



---

# Chapter 5

## **Chapter 5**

The koppie was a dark tooth against the greying dawn, its slopes strewn with boulders that looked like the slumped forms of sleeping giants. Eleanor walked towards it with Bheki and Samuel a pace behind, the three of them a silent, small procession in the vast bowl of land. The air was cold and carried the scent of dry grass and distant woodsmoke. In her coat pocket, the stone was a leaden weight, its carved surface worn smooth by guilt and time.

She had not slept. The hours after Bheki’s confession had been spent at the kitchen table, the stone on the scarred wood between them, while Samuel kept a grim vigil at the window. They had spoken in low, practical tones, mapping the few paths left. To keep the stone was to hold a lit fuse. To reveal it was to invite the storm. Mkize’s meeting, set for sunrise at the foot of the eastern koppie, was now an arraignment. They were not walking to a parley. They were walking into a verdict.

“The path splits ahead,” Bheki said, his voice rough from the silence. “The main track goes to the meeting ground. There is another way. A older way.”

Eleanor followed his gaze to a seam in the rockface, half-hidden by a thornbrake that clutched the stone with skeletal fingers. It was not a path, but a refusal of one. “Where does it lead?”

“Up,” he said simply. “To the high place. Where the *izinyanga*, the healers, would go for vision. It overlooks the meeting ground.”

“A spy-hole,” Samuel muttered, his hand resting on the worn grip of his rifle. “Useful.”

“It is not for spying,” Bheki corrected, a faint edge in his tone. “It is for seeing.”

The choice was a quiet one. The main track was broad, worn by generations of feet, leading to the communal fire, to the collective eye. The seam was a secret, a climb towards solitude and perspective. Mkize had set his stage below. But the test, Eleanor felt in her bones, was not down there in the gathering crowd. It was here, in this fork. To choose the expected path was to accept the role of the supplicant, the colonist called to account. To choose the hidden one was to claim something else—a different kind of authority, stolen and perilous.

“We take the older way,” she said.

Bheki gave a single, slow nod, something like relief passing over his features. He moved first, parting the thorns with a careful, practiced hand, holding the vicious branches back for her and Samuel. The entrance was a crack just wide enough to shoulder through, the rock cool and gritty against her cheek. Inside, the world narrowed to a steep, twisting chimney of stone, the light from behind them fading to a dull glow. The air smelled of dust and age and something mineral, like cold breath from deep in the earth.

They climbed. It was not a puzzle of intellect, but of body and will. The rock demanded concessions: a scrabbling grip where there was no hold, a contortion through a tight squeeze, a leap across a black fissure that echoed with the drip of hidden water. It was a sacrifice of dignity, of certainty. Eleanor’s hands grew raw, her breath came in short, sharp gasps that echoed in the confined space. Samuel, behind her, laboured with his rifle, the metal scraping against stone with a sound that set her teeth on edge. Only Bheki moved with a fluid economy, his shape a shadow ahead, pausing now and then to reach back a hand she often refused.

Halfway up, the chimney opened into a shallow cave, a natural resting place. A faint, grey light seeped in from a high crack. On the sandy floor, arranged in a neat circle, were seven smooth river stones. In the centre of the circle lay a small, dried bundle of herbs tied with a strip of faded hide.

Bheki stopped as if struck. He did not enter the space.

“What is this?” Eleanor whispered, her voice loud in the stillness.

“A *umsamo*,” he said. “A small shrine. For the ancestors of this place.” He looked at the bundle, his face unreadable. “It is fresh. Days, not weeks.”

“Mkize?” Samuel asked, peering past her shoulder.

“Or one of his people. It is a guardian. Not of the path, but of the silence here.” Bheki turned to Eleanor. “To pass, we must acknowledge it. Or we must turn back.”

“Acknowledge how?”

“That is the puzzle. Do we add something? Do we take nothing? Do we speak a name?” He spread his hands. “I am not of this specific earth. My ancestors are from the coast. My respect may be the wrong kind.”

Eleanor looked at the humble circle. It was not a barrier of force, but of belonging. To bypass it ignorantly would be a profound insult, a confirmation of every colonial charge of blindness. To fake reverence would be worse. She thought of the stone in her pocket, the stolen heart. That was a violation of a different scale, but born of the same root: taking without understanding.

She knelt, ignoring the protest of her knees on the hard floor. She did not reach for the bundle or the stones. Instead, she took from her other pocket the only thing she had that felt remotely like an offering: a single, weathered button from her father’s old shooting jacket, brass, stamped with the faint image of a gull. It was a trivial thing, a piece of a dead man’s costume from a dead world. But it was all she had that was truly hers to give. She placed it just outside the circle of stones, a foreign object at the threshold of the sacred.

“I have nothing of worth to give you,” she said to the quiet air. “Only this proof that I am a stranger here. And that I see this place is not empty.”

She stood. Bheki was watching her, his eyes dark pools in the dim light. After a moment, he stepped forward, not into the circle, but beside her. He bent and, from a small pouch at his belt, sprinkled a pinch of coarse, dark tobacco over her button. A libation of shared incompleteness.

They moved past the *umsamo*. The guardian silence remained, but it was no longer barred against them.

The final ascent was shorter, the light growing stronger. They emerged onto a narrow ledge, a lip of rock fringed with tough, wind-stunted shrubs. Below and to the east, the meeting ground was spread out like a living map. Fifty, perhaps sixty people were gathered in a loose semicircle, men, women, a few children held close. Their voices were a low murmur that rose on the cold air like the hum of a hive. In the centre, seated on a folded kaross, was Mkize. Even from this distance, his posture was one of immense, patient stillness.

The sight was a fist around Eleanor’s heart. This was the reality of the aftermath: not a faceless mob, but a community. Faces she vaguely recognised from the farm’s periphery—the young man who sometimes brought word from the store, the woman who sold eggs, the old shepherd who drifted with his ragged flock along the borderlands. They were not an army. They were a jury. And she was in the dock, unseen, above them.

“He will wait until the sun clears the ridge,” Bheki said, crouching beside a boulder. “He will speak of the land, of the drought, of the broken promises. Then he will call for you.”

“And when I do not appear?” Eleanor asked, her eyes fixed on the scene.

“He will send men to the farm. They will find it empty. He will then state the truth he has already shaped: that you have fled, confirming your guilt. That the land is forfeit. The meeting will become a planning council. The moral pressure becomes physical claim.”

It was, as Bheki had foretold, a perfect mechanism. Her absence would be as damning as any confession.

“So we watch our own defeat,” Samuel said, bitterness etching his words. “A fine climb for a fine view.”

But Eleanor was not listening. She was tracing the lines of the koppie itself, the way the morning shadows fell from its western face. The hidden path had not led them to a spy-hole, but to a threshold. The real cave was not below. It was here, inside her, and it demanded entry.

“This is not the high place,” she said, turning to Bheki. “The vision place. This is just a ledge. Where is it?”

He held her gaze for a long moment, then gestured with his chin towards the rear of the ledge. Where it met the main cliff face, there was a deeper shadow, an overhang that promised a cavity behind. “There. It is small. A crack in the thought of the mountain.”

“I’m going in.”

“Eleanor—” Samuel began, but she was already moving.

The overhang sheltered a short, sloping tunnel. She had to drop to her hands and knees, the stone scraping her palms. The darkness was absolute, swallowing the sound of her breathing. For a few blind yards, she crawled, the weight of the mountain above her a palpable pressure. Then the tunnel opened.

The space was no larger than the pantry at Willowbrook. A faint, diffused light came from some unseen fissure high above, giving the air a underwater gloom. The floor was fine, dry sand. And the walls…

She raised a hand, her fingers trembling.

They were not plain rock. They were covered in paintings. Ochre red, chalk white, charcoal black. Her breath caught. A herd of eland flowed along one wall, their bodies monumental and graceful. A line of stylised human figures danced, arms raised. A complex geometric pattern, like a honeycomb or a net, spread across the ceiling. And in the centre of the far wall, larger than all the rest, was a figure. Part human, part… something else. It stood with one hand raised to a sunburst, the other holding a shape that could have been a vessel, or a heart.

This was the cave. Not a hiding place for treasure, but a cathedral of memory. A deep fear uncoiled in her gut—not of the dark or of confinement, but of profound insignificance. Her family’s drama, the stolen stone, the farm deeds, the colonial law—it was all a shallow, recent scratch on the surface of this. This place held a truth that predated Chadwicks, predated colonies, predated history itself. The shocking truth from her past was that she *had* no past here. She was a ghost, a flicker. The weight of her guilt was trivial compared to the weight of time in this silent room.

She heard a scuff behind her. Samuel’s voice, muffled and strained. “Eleanor? You must come out. Now.”

She turned, crawling back towards the pinprick of light. As she emerged, squinting, Samuel pointed down to the meeting ground.

A shift had occurred. Two men were walking away from the crowd, heading with purpose towards the main track to Willowbrook. Mkize was standing now, his voice, though still indistinct, carried a new, formal rhythm. He was beginning the narrative of her absence.

“They are coming,” Samuel said, his rifle half-raised. “We have minutes.”

Bheki looked from the descending men to Eleanor’s face, which was pale and strangely calm. “What did you see?”

“The reason for the stone,” she said. Her voice was hollow, rinsed clean by the awe in the cave. “It wasn’t a key. It was a… a piece of a story. One they kept alive. And we took it to put in a drawer.” She looked at Bheki, the betrayal of the night before now reframed. “You didn’t just let me walk into a test. You led me to the altar.”

Before he could answer, a sharp crack echoed off the koppie. Not a gunshot. The sound of splitting wood. Then a shout, from a different direction—from the farm.

Samuel spun. “The homestead!”

A curl of dark smoke began to rise from the cluster of farm buildings, a thick, rising stain against the morning blue. It was a diversion, or a punishment. Or both. The men Mkize had sent were a feint. Others had already been deployed.

“They are burning the outbuildings,” Bheki said, his voice tight. “The storehouse. The stable.”

Willowbrook. Her last shred of power, her standing, going up in smoke. The trap was not binary. It was a vise with many jaws.

“We must go,” Samuel said, already moving towards the chimney descent. “Now, Eleanor!”

Bheki did not follow him. He looked at Eleanor, then at the smoke. “If you go down, you run towards the fire. You will be caught between it and the men on the path. You will be reacting. You will lose.”

The allies were separating, not by force, but by the fundamental divergence of their purpose. Samuel’s duty was to property, to a tangible legacy. Bheki’s was to the land, to an intangible truth. Eleanor stood between them, the stone burning in her pocket.

“Go, Samuel,” she said. “Save what you can.”

The old man stared at her, his face a mask of anguish and fury. “It is your *life* up here!”

“It is my life down there, too,” she said. “But it is a different life. Go.”

With a last, devastated look, Samuel turned and disappeared into the chimney, his descent a clatter of desperation.

Eleanor and Bheki were alone on the ledge. The sound of the meeting below was a distant wave. The smoke from her home pillared into the sky.

“What is left?” she asked, the emptiness of the cave still inside her.

“The meeting,” Bheki said. “But not as a supplicant. Not as a fleeing thief.” He nodded towards the painted cave behind her. “You have seen the deep past. It has broken you from your own. Now you must speak from the break.”

“They will tear me apart.”

“Perhaps. Or perhaps you will speak a language Mkize does not expect. The language of the land is not only justice. It is also grief. Show him you have learned its grammar.”

The preparation was not martial. It was an excavation. She sat on the cold stone, the rising sun finally spilling over the ridge and painting the ledge in gold and sharp shadow. She took the stone from her pocket and laid it on the rock between them. She thought not of courtrooms or deeds, but of the painted eland, of the dancers, of the figure holding the vessel to the sun. She thought of her father’s button, left at the shrine. She thought of the bindweed in the Lincolnshire kitchen garden, a stubborn, living thing she had torn from the earth, year after year. She was the bindweed here. A stubborn, living thing, out of place, doing damage simply by existing.

To be stripped of every prop—the farm, the ally, the law, the moral high ground—was terrifying. It was also a kind of brutal freedom. When there was nothing left to defend, you could only ever tell the truth.

She stood. The sun was fully up. The meeting below was at its peak, Mkize’s voice rising in a final, declarative cadence. The moment for her condemnation had arrived.

“How do I get down?” she asked Bheki.

He led her to the far end of the ledge, where a series of natural steps, like a rough staircase, were cut into the rockface, leading down to the very edge of the meeting ground. It was not a hidden path. It was a ceremonial descent, emerging beside a large, solitary baobab tree, its branches like gnarled roots reaching into the sky.

“I cannot go with you,” he said at the bottom, concealed still by the bulk of the tree. “This you do alone.”

She nodded, the stone cold and heavy in her hand. She looked at him, this divided man, this guide to mirrors and traps. “Thank you,” she said. It was not forgiveness. It was acknowledgment.

Then she stepped out from behind the baobab.

The shift in the air was immediate. A hundred faces turned towards her. The murmuring died into a silence so complete she could hear the crackle of the central fire. She saw the shock on the faces of the two men who had been sent to fetch her, now frozen at the edge of the crowd. She saw Mkize, his sentence halted mid-breath, his eyes narrowing not in anger, but in profound reassessment.

She walked into the centre of the semicircle, the open ground between her and Mkize feeling like a mile of exposed, hard earth. She did not look at the crowd. She looked only at him, and at the land behind him.

She stopped. She held out her hand, and opened her fingers.

The carved stone lay on her palm, catching the morning light.

“I found the heart of the land,” she said, her voice clear and carrying in the stillness. “It was in my father’s desk. Where he put the things he took and could not bear to look at.” She paused, letting the words settle like dust. “I bring it back. Not to buy forgiveness. Not to keep my farm.” She looked at the stone, then at Mkize. “I bring it back because it is the one true thing I have touched here. And I am tired of holding lies.”

Mkize said nothing. His gaze moved from the stone to her face, searching for the strategy, the trick. He found only the hollowed-out aftermath of the cave.

“You climb the old way,” he said finally, his voice low. “You come from the high place. Why?”

“To see,” Eleanor said, echoing Bheki’s words. “And to be seen.”

A woman in the crowd, older, her face a map of deep lines, spoke. “The smoke. You burn your own?”

“I did not light it,” Eleanor said, meeting the woman’s eyes. “But the tinder was mine. My absence. My debt. Let it burn. It is just a building. This,” she raised the stone slightly, “is not.”

The ground beneath her did not feel unstable anymore. It felt like what it was: solid, ancient, indifferent. She had stepped into the hungry darkness, and it had swallowed her whole. The woman who owned Willowbrook Farm was gone, burned away with the outbuildings. What stood in the meeting ground was something else, something raw and unrecognisable, holding out a stolen heart and waiting for the world to decide if it was a weapon, a key, or simply a stone.

Mkize rose to his feet. He did not reach for the offering. He took a step towards her, his shadow falling across the dust. The life-or-death confrontation was not one of violence, but of recognition. He was no longer looking at a colonial daughter. He was looking at a fellow casualty of history, holding a piece of the wreckage.

“Now,” he said softly, so only she could hear, “the true trial begins.”



---

# Chapter 6

## **Chapter 6**

The trial was not a council. It was a walking.

Mkize turned from her, his movement a dismissal of the charged silence, and began to walk east, away from the meeting ground, towards the line of low, blue hills that humped against the sky like the spines of sleeping beasts. He did not look back. The message was in the set of his shoulders, in the deliberate, unhurried pace: follow, or do not. The choice, like every choice that had led her to this dust, was hers alone.

Eleanor looked at the stone in her palm, its carved channels dark with the sweat of her skin. She closed her fingers around it once, a final, private farewell, then slipped it into the pocket of her trousers. The fabric was stiff with dried mud and salt from the cave. She fell into step behind him, her own boots raising small, apologive puffs of dust. The crowd parted without a word, their faces closed and watchful. The older woman who had spoken of the smoke gave a slow, almost imperceptible nod. It was not encouragement. It was an acknowledgement of a path entered.

They walked for an hour, perhaps two. Time stretched and thinned in the heat, measured only by the rhythm of their footfalls and the slow wheel of the sun. Mkize did not speak. He led her along cattle paths that were faint scars on the land, through dry riverbeds where the stones were bone-white and round, past stands of thorn trees that clutched at the sky with desperate, blackened fingers. The landscape was a vast, breathing entity. It was not the flat, green surrender of the fens; it was a coiled, muscular thing, all rise and fall, secret hollow and exposed ridge. It watched.

Willowbrook was gone. The thought came to her not as a pang, but as a fact, clean and sharp as a splinter of glass. The deed in the iron box, the ledgers with their columns of profit and loss, the kitchen garden with its bindweed—all of it was ash on the wind. The freedom was terrifying. It was the freedom of a bird pushed from the nest, not into flight, but into the sheer, gut-churning drop.

Mkize stopped at the crest of a rise. Below them lay a valley, different from the one that held the village. This was a place of older wounds. The earth was scarred with the geometric ghosts of foundations—straight lines and right angles that blasphemed against the land’s curves. Rusted iron teeth of machinery protruded from the red soil. A chimney stack of crumbling brick stood sentinel over nothing, its shadow a stark finger pointing at the valley’s heart. This was the mine. The *other* mine, the one her father’s consortium had opened and exhausted and abandoned within five years, leaving a gutted place.

“Your enemy is not me,” Mkize said, his first words since the walking began. His voice was flat, drained of the meeting-ground’s performative gravity. “Your enemy is here. The ghost in the ground. The hunger that was not sated.”

Eleanor stared at the ruins. They were not majestic in their decay. They were sordid, like the discarded bones of a cheap meal. “This is the trial? To look at my father’s folly?”

“No.” He pointed a long finger not at the ruins, but at the far end of the valley, where the land began to climb again. There, nestled in a fold of hills, was a cluster of proper buildings—a farmstead, by the look of it, but grander than those in the village. A whitewashed homestead, a barn of corrugated iron, several outbuildings. A thread of smoke rose from a chimney. It looked ordered, prosperous, defiantly out of place. “Your enemy is there. Jan van der Pol. He bought the mining rights from your father’s company when they left. He bought the land from the government, when the government decided it could sell what it did not own. He runs cattle. He has a borehole that drains the water from three springs that fed our lands. He has a rifle, and men who use them.”

The pieces shifted, locking into a new and uglier configuration. This was not about a stolen ritual stone. This was about a stolen water table. Her father’s legacy was not a single act of theft, but a chain of them, each link sold to the next bidder.

“He is a farmer,” Eleanor said, the word tasting like lime on her tongue.

“He is a settler,” Mkize corrected. “He believes the land is his to take. He believes we are a nuisance on it. Last season, his men shot two of our boys who were searching for a stray calf near his fence. They said they thought they were jackals.” Mkize finally turned to look at her. His eyes were the colour of the dry earth. “You bring back a stone. Good. The heart is here. But the body is bleeding. Your enemy is the man who holds the knife. You wish to be seen? Go. Be seen by him.”

***
